[3:04] one line, sudden versus gradual, is where tens of thousands of dollars get won or lost. So, let's break down step-by-step how to stay on the right side of it. Point one, understand the exact line the adjuster is standing on when they look at your claim because they're sorting it into one of two buckets. Bucket number one, sudden and accidental. A pipe freezes overnight and splits. A washing machine supply hose

[3:34] lets go while you're at work. A water heater ruptures and dumps 40 gallons of water on the floor immediately and it keeps running because that supply line isn't stopping. That's fast. It's unexpected and it's normally on the covered side. Bucket two, mhm that's a different bucket. Gradual wear and tear. A slow drip behind the wall that rots out the framing over a year.

[4:04] A fitting that's been weeping a little bit for months. Corrosion has been building and nobody dealt with it. That's the excluded side. And it doesn't matter how much damage it did. The cause is what gets denied. Here's the part that catches good people off guard. The exact same puddle on the floor can get ruled either way, depending entirely on what the evidence shows about how long it had been happening. Now, I've watched

[4:34] a homeowner have a real, legitimate sudden failure and lose the claim anyway because the surrounding pipe had old corrosion on it. And the adjuster, he decided the whole thing was gradual. Same water on the floor, completely different outcome. And it came down to the story that the evidence told. So, here's what really happened. Guys, a lot of times when this copper's put in, if the plumber doesn't wipe the

[5:04] flux, it turns green. It looks like it's corroded. It's not, it's oxidation. There's a lot of problems there that appraisers don't necessarily understand, the adjusters don't understand, but this is them making a decision based on what it looks like. Now, this is exactly why what you do in the first hour before you clean up a single thing matters more than almost anything in the world. Okay, so point number two, document it like the claim depends on it because it absolutely does. And almost

[5:36] nobody does this part right because the first instinct is to grab towels and start cleaning. No, not yet. The second you find water, before you touch anything, get your phone out. Take photos. Take video. Wide shots, so you can see the whole room, close-ups. Close-ups of the source. Close-ups of the damage. More is better. You can't go back in time and reshoot something, so get as many video and photos as you can.

[6:08] Then, shut the water off. At the fixture stop, if you can reach it and if that will stop the flow. At the main, if you can't. And here's a little thing that matters. Your phone is already stamping every one of these photos with a date and time. Now, let me give you a little tip here. When you're videoing, "This water was not here last night when I went to bed. This suddenly appeared this morning." It's factual. Make sure it's part of the video. That is part of

[6:38] your message. Now, this is your best friend when somebody's trying to argue that this was slow. When you say, "Look, this wasn't here yesterday. How do you think this is slow?" Learn how to look at it and document it. Now, this next one is the one that people blow. Save the part that failed. If you call a plumber, don't let him throw anything away. The burst hose, the cracked fitting, the split section of pipe, do not throw it in the trash. That physical piece of metal or rubber,

[7:10] that's your evidence that the failure was sudden. An adjuster can argue with your words. It's a lot harder to argue with the actual broken part sitting right there in a ziploc bag. And then, report it promptly. I know life gets busy, but a big gap between when it happened and when you called it in is just one more thing that somebody can point to you and call it gradual. Quick is your friend here. You move quick, just like the leak did. So, reframe the

[7:40] whole thing in your head. You're not just cleaning up a mess, you're building a case. The case that this was sudden. This was accidental. And this is exactly what you pay your insurance company for. Point three, and this is the five-minute fix that I told you about. The single cheapest thing you can do to keep yourself on the covered side in the first place. That's where you want to be, on the covered side. Go look behind your washing machine right now.

[8:10] Pause the video if you need to. If you see a black rubber old hose behind there, you know, the old black ones that they've hooked up washing machines with forever, those are one of the most common causes of catastrophic water damages in an entire house. Here's why. Those hoses are under constant water pressure, 24 hours a day, whether the machine is running or not. Rubber gets old. It gets brittle. And one day, one of them just lets go. And

[8:42] if it lets go on a Saturday while you're out running errands, while you're out buying pellets for your smoker and a brisket to throw on it, it can put hundreds of gallons of water on the floor before anybody even realizes it, before anybody can get in there and shut it off. I've seen that one flood a second-story house, a condo, and come through the ceiling downstairs. I've seen a busted toilet upstairs flood an entire upstairs and downstairs of a

[9:12] condo. Guys, keep an eye on things, okay? The fix is simple. If you look behind your washing machine and it's a black rubber hose, go buy the braided stainless steel kind. The braided stainless steel hoses, they're a few bucks more each, and you can get them at any big box store. They thread on the exact same way, and it's a five-minute job with the pressure analog. Turn off the valve right there behind the washing machine in the washer box. Take the old ones

[9:43] off, put the new ones on. Make sure hot goes to hot, cold goes to cold. Then turn it back on. Check for any drips. Done. That's the whole thing. That's the 5-minute fix that's going to save you from having most of the water damages that a lot of people have. And while you've got the bug to fix stuff, knock out the rest of the list. Check your ice maker line. Is it stainless steel or is it just rubber? You know, the one behind the fridge. Those little plastic ones are notorious for breaking. [10:14] your sinks, under your kitchen sink. And you always need to know how old your water heater is because most of them have a real lifespan. Somewhere around 8 years. If you've been flushing it, you might get a little more. Most of them have a warranty for six. They'll normally last eight. Eight to 12 if you do things right. [6:40] leak detection, it all has a price written next to it. Same price for every customer. I don't care how nice the house is, or how horrible it is. Same price your tech reads off of the iPad. [7:10] what? Your competition is higher because they understand what it actually cost to work in your town. Number two, this price list makes your techs interchangeable. That way I know when I put my flux on, it's going to be on clean copper and it's going to help draw that solder in. Fitting brush, you really want these. Now, if you've got an old one or you got one that's not getting it good and clean, I've seen people take abrasive cloth,

[5:14] fold it up on a half round like that, stick their finger inside. It's a little bit more work, I get it. But if you look inside, you can get just as clean of an edge. Maybe even cleaner than the other. Oh, I didn't do the other. I did the same one. But either way, you want to make sure you've got it nice and clean. Okay, so

[5:44] step two is applying flux. Now, flux is a chemical cleaner that allows the solder to bond with the copper. The secret here is a very thin coat, a very thin even coat. Too much flux will burn and prevent a good seal. Now, you just need enough to make the copper look wet. Apply it to both the pipe and the inside of the fitting. And it's really easy to do. You've got a little bitty fitting brush here and you just need a little bit on here.

[6:14] It doesn't take a lot. And I paint it on like Picasso, man. I just I put it on. I want to make sure that I come down to the bottom of the shiny part where I have it cleaned. And then on the fitting, look up inside there and do the exact same thing. Now, if you look in there, it's just a very, very thin coat

[6:44] and it goes all the way around. And you can look down in it. I've gone down just past the middle. So, what I'm going to do is go ahead and slide that on and go down. Now, these are repair couplings so there is not a stop on the inside. Those that have a stop, you literally push it in until it stops and that's exactly what it's designed for. So now I'm going to do the flux again on the other piece. And again, it's just a very thin, very

[7:15] consistent coat all the way around. Now, I do want to make sure that the flux goes all the way down to the other piece of pipe because I want the solder to draw in all the way to where these two pieces of pipes butt up. Now, these pieces I can feel on the inside are touching, which is exactly what I want. Now, me,

[7:48] I'm a little retentive. You see the straps, you see the letters? To me, you can take just a second, just like I did, and make sure those are lined up. Now, if I were installing these in a wall, I would want those letters to face outside. That way when the inspector walks up, he can just look down and he knows exactly what he's dealing with. He knows whether it's by code or not. So, I'm going to go ahead and put this in the vise and get it ready to solder and then we'll talk about one of the

[8:18] most important steps. Okay, so step three is the heat. And here's the number one mistake beginners make. They heat the pipe. You don't heat the pipe, you heat the fitting. The fitting is thicker and holds more heat. Heat the fitting and the fitting will heat the pipe. You'll know it's hot enough when the flux starts to sizzle and smoke just a little bit. Now, me, there are times that I heat the pipe first. I may heat the pipe if I'm worried about it moving or shifting or I'm trying to keep

[8:48] something level. I'll heat the pipe to expand the pipe. Now, I don't get the pipe red glowing hot. I want to then bring my heat in here. And one of the other problems is the size of the tip. Look at the flame on this. Now, you see that? That is a lot more fire than you're going to need for this. So, what you want to make sure you do is use the right tip. Now, people tend to burn a fitting up. They'll use way too

[9:20] much heat, they'll cook the flux out, they'll do a lot of different things. But this, that's a big enough flame. Okay, so again, me, if I don't want it to move, I may go ahead and just kind of heat the pipe up just enough to expand it. Now, as I heat the fitting itself, in this case the coupling, everything's going to kind of expand together and I'm not going to have to worry about anything. Then, we take the solder, we put it in, draw it to the middle and call it good.

[9:54] >> [snorts] >> And now the magic. Step four is applying the solder. Touch the tip of your solder to the joint on the side opposite the flame. If the fitting is hot enough, it will instantly melt and the solder will be sucked into the joint by capillary action. You'll see a silver ring appear all the way around. That's it. Remove the solder, then the heat, and let it cool

[10:24] down. Now one thing I like to do as a plumber is always have a good clean rag or soldering gloves on and just bump the bottom of it just a little bit to make sure that there's not any solder build up, any drip leg, anything at all like that. The final step is to wipe the joint with a semi-damp rag, not while it's hot. Now this removes excessive flux and gives you that clean professional look. This is something a lot of young kids don't do anymore. They

[10:54] were never taught the right way. I didn't use a lot of pressure on it. I'm not trying to push the fitting. To be honest, this is still hot. I could actually grab that pipe and probably pull the joint apart or make it crack because it's not cooled down yet. Now, there's a lot of people I know that will take a cold wet rag at this point and wipe it down. I don't like doing that. Even if I'm in a position where I know I need it to cure quick because I need to test it or something, I try not to do that. I try to let it cool just

[11:25] naturally. Now there are some people that will come through and spray them down and soap them down from the get-go. I want to wait till it cools down a little bit. But after it cools down, I may take clean water and put on it. I may just take a good clean rag and wipe it off. Now, I've talked long enough. It's probably cooled down enough. So just going ahead and wiping it now, what that will do, it will get any excess flux off of there. And to me, it's just going to make it look like a better joint. What you're left with is a perfectly strong, leak-proof joint that

[11:57] will last for hundreds of years. Mastering a core skill like this is the first step on the path to an amazing career. It's a feeling of pride. It's a feeling of accomplishment.
